Deciding Confluence and Normal Form Properties of Ground Term Rewrite Systems Efficiently Thanks: This research was supported by FWF (Austrian Science Fund) project P27528.
Bertram Felgenhauer

Abstract
. It is known that the first-order theory of rewriting is decidable for ground term rewrite systems, but the general technique uses tree automata and often takes exponential time. For many properties, including confluence ( CR ), uniqueness of normal forms with respect to reductions ( UNR ) and with respect to conversions ( UNC ), polynomial time decision procedures are known for ground term rewrite systems. However, this is not the case for the normal form property ( NFP ). In this work, we present a cubic time algorithm for NFP , an almost cubic time algorithm for UNR , and an almost linear time algorithm for UNC , improving previous bounds. We also present a cubic time algorithm for CR .



Oct. 31, 2017Oct. 29, 2018

\titlecomment

The decision procedure for confluence of ground term rewrite systems previously appeared at RTA 2012.

1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
F.2 Analysis of Algorithms and Problem Complexity,
F.4 Mathematical Logic and Formal Languages

1.
Introduction

In this article, we consider four properties of finite ground term rewrite systems,
that is, first-order term rewrite systems (TRSs) without variables.
These properties are

•

confluence or, equivalently, the Church-Rosser property (
CR
),
which states that any two convertible terms have a common reduct;

•

the normal form property (
NFP
),
which holds if any term convertible to a normal form can be reduced
to that normal form;

•

uniqueness of normal forms with respect to conversions (
UNC
),
meaning that any two convertible normal forms are equal; and

•

uniqueness of normal forms with respect to reductions (
UNR
),
stating that from any term, at most one normal form can be reached.

In seminal work
[
4
]
,
Dauchet and Tison established that
the first-order theory of ground term rewrite systems is decidable
using tree automata techniques.
This result is applicable to all four properties.
While the procedure is usually exponential,
it yields polynomial time procedures for
UNC
and
UNR
with a bit of care.
This is elaborated in Section
8.1
.

In fact it is known that
CR
,
UNC
, and
UNR
are decidable in polynomial time for ground TRSs.
In this article,
we are interested in bounding the exponent of the associated polynomials,
which is of importance to implementers.

As far as we know, the best previous result for
UNC
is an almost quadratic algorithm
by Verma et al.
[
22
]
with
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2}\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time complexity,
where
\|\mathcal{R}\|
denotes the sum of the sizes of the rules of
\mathcal{R}
,
and the size of a rule is the sum of the sizes
of its left-hand side and right-hand side.
In Section
4
we present an algorithm that decides
UNC
in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.
In the case of
UNR
for ground TRSs,
Verma
[
21
]
and Godoy and Jacquemard
[
7
]
have established
that polynomial time algorithms exist, using tree automata techniques.
No precise bound is given by these authors.
In Section
5
we present an
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3}\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time algorithm for deciding
UNR
.
Furthermore we present a
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
decision procedure for
NFP
for ground TRSs, which will be covered in Section
6
.
As far as we know, this is the first polynomial time decision procedure
for
NFP
in the literature.
Last but not least,
we present a
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
decision procedure for
CR
,
closely based on previous work by the author
[
6
]
.
See Section
7
for details.
In Section
8
we discuss related work,
and we conclude in Section
9
.

2.
Preliminaries

We assume familiarity with term rewriting and (bottom-up) tree automata.
For an overview of term rewriting, see
[
1
]
;
for tree automata, please refer to
[
2
]
.
We recall the notions used in this article.

A signature is a set of function symbols

\Sigma
each associated with an arity (which are natural numbers).
The ground terms
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
over
\Sigma
are constructed inductively in
the usual way: If
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
and

f\in\Sigma
has arity
n
,
then
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
. A position
p
of a term is a
sequence of natural numbers addressing a subterm
t|_{p}
.
Replacement of subterms
t[u]_{p}
, and the size of terms
|t|
have
their standard definitions
[
1
]
. A term
t
together with a
position
p
defines a context
C[\cdot]=t[\cdot]_{p}
.
Contexts can be instantiated,
C[s]=t[s]_{p}
.
Alternatively, contexts
C
can be viewed as terms that contain an
extra constant
\square
, representing a hole, exactly once.
Then
C[t]
denotes the result of replacing
\square
by
t
.
Multi-hole contexts are terms that may contain several holes;
for a multi-hole context
C
with
n
occurrences of
\square
,

C[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]
denotes the term obtained by replacing the holes
in
C
by
t_{1}
to
t_{n}
from left to right.
Function symbols with arity
0
are called constants.
A ground term is flat if it is either a constant
or a function symbol applied to constants.

A set
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)^{2}
of rules is a ground term rewrite system (ground TRS).
If
(\ell,r)\in\mathcal{R}
, we also write
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
.
By
\mathcal{R}^{-}
,
\mathcal{R}^{\pm}
,
|\mathcal{R}|
,
\|\mathcal{R}\|
we denote
\mathcal{R}^{-1}

(i.e., the inverse of
\mathcal{R}
, where we view
\mathcal{R}
as a relation on ground terms),

\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{R}^{-1}
, the number of rules in
\mathcal{R}
, and
the total size of the rules,
\sum_{\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}}(|\ell|+|r|)
,
respectively. Any ground TRS
\mathcal{R}
induces a rewrite relation
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}

on ground terms:

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}t
if there is a context
C[\cdot]
such that

C[\ell]=s
and
C[r]=t
for some
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
.
Properties like flatness extend to rules and TRSs.
For example, a rule is left-flat if its left-hand side is flat; a TRS is
left-flat if all its rules are.
We write
t\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}
if
t
is a subterm of a side of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
.

Given a rewrite relation
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}
, we denote by
\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{}}{\mathrel{\quad\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 7.97224pt\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 6.80565pt\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}
,
\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{}}{\leftrightarrow}{\leftrightarrow}{\leftrightarrow}
,

\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{=}}{\rightarrow^{=}}{\rightarrow^{=}}{\rightarrow^{=}}
,
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}
, its inverse, symmetric closure,
reflexive closure, reflexive transitive closure, respectively,
and
\cdot
composes rewrite relations.
A term
s
is a normal form (w.r.t.
\to
)
if there is no
t
with
s\to t
.
By
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}
we denote reduction to normal form:
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}t
if

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}t
and
t
is a normal form with respect to
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}
.
Two terms
s
and
t
are convertible if
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}t
. They are joinable,
denoted by
s\downarrow t
, if
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 14.59724pt\hskip-14.59724pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-14.59724pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.15283pt\hskip-12.15283pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-12.15283pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 10.98624pt\hskip-10.98624pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-10.98624pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
.
If we have two rewrite relations,
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[1]{}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}
and
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[2]{}}{\rightarrow_{2}}{\rightarrow_{2}}{\rightarrow_{2}}
, then

{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[1]{}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}}/{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[2]{}}{\rightarrow_{2}}{\rightarrow_{2}}{\rightarrow_{2}}}
is defined as
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[2]{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[1]{}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\rightarrow_{1}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[2]{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{2}^{*}}
;
applied to TRSs, we write
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{S}
for the relative TRS
that induces
{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}/{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{S}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}}}
as a rewrite relation.
Given a signature
\Sigma
, the parallel closure
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}

is defined inductively by

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}s\qquad s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}ts\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}t\qquad f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\lx@proof@logical@and s_{1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}t_{1}\dots s_{n}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}{\rightarrow^{\mnshortparallel}}t_{n}

Let us recall the four main properties of interest for this paper.
A rewrite relation
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}
has …

•

the Church-Rosser property (
CR
),
if
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}t
implies
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 14.59724pt\hskip-14.59724pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-14.59724pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.15283pt\hskip-12.15283pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-12.15283pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 10.98624pt\hskip-10.98624pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{*}$}\hskip-10.98624pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
for all
s
and
t
;

•

the normal form property (
NFP
),
if
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}t
with
t
in normal form implies
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}{\rightarrow^{*}}t
for all
s
and
t
;

•

unique normal forms with respect to conversion (
UNC
),
if
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}t
implies
s=t

for all normal forms
s
and
t
; and

•

unique normal forms with respect to reductions (
UNR
),
if
t\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{!}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.76392pt\hskip-12.76392pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-12.76392pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 10.48616pt\hskip-10.48616pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-10.48616pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 9.31956pt\hskip-9.31956pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-9.31956pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}u
implies
t=u
for all
s
,
t
and
u
.

We refer to the last three properties collectively as
normal form properties
.
It is well known (and easy to see) that

\text{CR}\implies\text{NFP}\implies\text{UNC}\implies\text{UNR}

The converse implications are false, as demonstrated by the standard examples in Figure
1
.
A rewrite relation
\to
is terminating if there are no infinite rewrite sequences

t_{0}\to t_{1}\to\dots
.
For terminating rewrite relations,
confluence and the three normal form properties are equivalent.
If
\to
is terminating and confluent,
then
s{\downarrow}
denotes the normal form of
s
.

\circ
\cdot
\circ
\neg\text{UNR}\phantom{\neg}
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\circ
\phantom{\neg}\text{UNR}\wedge\neg\text{UNC}
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\phantom{\neg}\text{UNC}\wedge\neg\text{NFP}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\phantom{\neg}\text{NFP}\wedge\neg\text{CR}
\circ
\cdot
CR

Figure 1.
Examples showing that

\top\mathrel{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\implies$}\hbox to10.27771pt{$\displaystyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\implies$}\hbox to10.27771pt{$\textstyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\implies$}\hbox to8.02771pt{$\scriptstyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\implies$}\hbox to10.27771pt{$\displaystyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\implies$}\hbox to10.27771pt{$\textstyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\implies$}\hbox to8.02771pt{$\scriptstyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\implies$}\hbox to6.52771pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\hfil\mkern 0.0mu/\mkern 2.0mu\hfil$}}}\bot
.

A tree automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,Q_{f},\Delta)
consists of a finite set of states
Q
disjoint from
\Sigma
,
a set of final states
Q_{f}\subseteq Q
,
and a set
\Delta
of transitions
f(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})\to q
and
\epsilon
-transitions
p\to q
,
where
f
is an
n
-ary function symbol and
q_{1},\dots,q_{n},p,q\in Q
.
A deterministic tree automaton is an automaton without
\epsilon
-transitions
whose transitions have distinct left-hand sides
(we do not require deterministic tree automata to be completely defined).
Note that
\Delta
can be viewed as a ground TRS over an extended signature that contains
Q
as constants.
We write
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{A}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}}
for
\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\Delta]{}}{\rightarrow_{\Delta}}{\rightarrow_{\Delta}}{\rightarrow_{\Delta}}
,
where we regard the transitions as rewrite rules.
The language accepted by
\mathcal{A}
is
L(\mathcal{A})=\{s\mid s\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)\text{, }q\in Q_{f}\text{ and }s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{A}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}}q\}
.

In the complexity analysis we make use of the fact that systems of Horn
clauses can be solved in linear time, see Dowling and Gallier
[
5
]
.
Their procedure finds the smallest solution of a set of Horn clauses,
in the sense that as few atoms as possible become true,
in time linear in the total size of the clauses.
This often allows an elegant description of algorithms that compute finite,
inductively defined sets.
For example, the transitive closure of
R\subseteq I\times I

can be specified by the inference rules

(p,q)\in R^{+}(p,q)\in R\qquad(p,r)\in R^{+}\lx@proof@logical@and(p,q)\in Rr\in I(q,r)\in R^{+}

The relation
R
and the set
I
are known in advance,
so we treat
(p,q)\in R
and
r\in I
as side conditions
that are either true or false.
On the other hand, the relation
R^{+}
is unknown,
so we treat
(p,q)\in R^{+}
for
p,q\in I
as atoms whose truth value should be derived by Horn inference.
There are
\mathcal{O}(|I|^{2})
Horn clauses for (
base
) and

\mathcal{O}(|I|^{3})
Horn clauses for (
trans
).
The size of the individual clauses is
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
so the transitive closure can be computed in cubic time.

Some of the algorithms presented in this article
use maximally shared terms for efficiency;
this idea is also known as hash consing.
In a maximally shared representation,
each ground term
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
is represented by a unique identifier
(e.g., a natural number, or a pointer into memory),
which can be mapped to
f
and the identifiers of
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
.
In order to maintain maximal sharing,
a lookup table mapping
f
and identifiers of
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}

to the identifier of
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
is required.
If the arity of
f
is bounded,
constructing maximally shared terms incurs a logarithmic overhead
compared to a direct construction.
Crucially though, comparing two maximally shared terms takes constant time.

Common Elements

input TRS (
\mathcal{R}
)
currying
(
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
)
flattening
(
\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{E}
)
normal forms
(
\mathcal{N}
)
congruence closure
(
\mathcal{C}
)
rewrite closure
(
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E}
)
CR

NFP

UNC

UNR

Figure 2.
Dependencies of preprocessing steps.

In this section, we present computations that are shared between
the decision procedures for the four properties
UNR
,
UNC
,
NFP
and
CR
.
The dependencies are as follows (see also Figure
2
).

•

Currying (Section
3.1
) and flattening (Section
3.2
)
are preparatory steps
used for all four properties.

•

In Section
3.3
,
we construct an automaton recognizing normal forms,
which is used for the three normal form properties
UNR
,
UNC
, and
NFP
.

•

Congruence closure (Section
3.4
) is used for
UNC
,
NFP
, and
CR
.

•

Rewrite closure (Section
3.5
)
features in the procedures for
UNR
,
NFP
and
CR
.

The dashed arrow from
UNC
to
NFP
in Figure
2
indicates that
the procedure for
NFP
is an extension of the procedure for
UNC
.

It is known that
CR
and
UNC
are preserved by signature extension
for any TRS
(this is a consequence of
CR
and
UNC
being modular
[
13
,
20
]
).
The same holds for
NFP
and
UNR
for left-linear TRSs
[
12
,
13
]
.
Since we are concerned with ground systems,
which are trivially left-linear,
this means that we may assume that the signature of the input TRS
consists exactly of the symbols occurring in the input TRS.
In particular, that signature is finite,
and its size is bounded by
\|\mathcal{R}\|
.

Remark
\thethm
.

Neither
NFP
nor
UNR
are preserved by signature extension in general.
Counterexamples have been given by Kennaway et al.
[
11
]
.
(It is noteworthy that these counterexamples are presented as
counterexamples to the preservation of
NFP
and
UNR
by currying;
the failure of signature extension is only mentioned in passing.)

Relatedly,
the properties
CR
,
NFP
,
UNC
,
UNR
are normally defined
on terms with variables instead of ground terms;
the variants where rewriting is restricted to ground terms
are called ground-
CR
etc.
Fortunately, for ground TRSs, the addition of variables
makes no difference.
This is because any counterexample to one of these properties
(which is a conversion
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}{\leftrightarrow^{*}}t
for
CR
,
NFP
, or
UNC
,
or a peak
t\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{!}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.76392pt\hskip-12.76392pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-12.76392pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 10.48616pt\hskip-10.48616pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-10.48616pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 9.31956pt\hskip-9.31956pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{!}$}\hskip-9.31956pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}{\rightarrow^{!}}u
in the case of
UNR
)
includes a counterexample with a root step
(which may be obtained by minimizing the size of
s
),
and any conversion (or peak) with a root step consists solely of
ground terms over the TRS’s inherent signature.
(This also shows that for ground TRSs, all four properties
(
CR
,
NFP
,
UNC
,
UNR
) are preserved by signature extension.)

Example
\thethm

(running example)
.

We demonstrate the constructions
on the following two ground TRSs
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{a}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{b}\to\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})))\to\mathsf{b}\}

3.1.
Currying

Currying turns an arbitrary TRS into one over constants and a single binary function symbol,
thereby bounding the maximum arity of the resulting TRS.

Definition
\thethm
.

In order to curry a ground TRS
\mathcal{R}
, we change all function symbols in
\Sigma
to be constants,
and add a fresh, binary function symbol
\circ
.
The resulting signature is
\Sigma^{\circ}=\Sigma\cup\{{\circ}\}
.
We write
\circ
as a left-associative infix operator
(i.e.,
s\circ t
stands for
{\circ}(s,t)
,
and
s\circ t\circ u=(s\circ t)\circ u\neq s\circ(t\circ u)
).
The operation
t^{\circ}
that curries a term
t
is defined inductively
by the equation

(f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))^{\circ}=f\circ t_{1}^{\circ}\circ\cdots\circ t_{n}^{\circ}

The
curried version of
\mathcal{R}
is given by
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}=\{\ell^{\circ}\to r^{\circ}\mid\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}\}
.

For ground systems, currying reflects and preserves the normal form properties and confluence.
For reflection, a direct simulation argument works
(
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}t
holds if and only if
s^{\circ}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}t^{\circ}
,
the image of
{-}^{\circ}
is closed under rewriting by
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
and
s^{\circ}
is an
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form if and only if
s
is an
\mathcal{R}
-normal form).
For preservation, Kenneway et al.
[
11
]
show that
UNR
and
NFP
are preserved by currying for left-linear systems,
and that
UNC
is preserved by currying for arbitrary TRSs.
Kahrs
[
10
]
shows that currying preserves confluence of TRSs.
In the case that
\mathcal{R}
is finite,
currying can be performed in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.
The resulting TRS is at most twice as large as the original TRS,
which follows from the inequality
|s^{\circ}|\leq 2|s|-1

that can be shown by induction on
s
.

Example
\thethm

(continued from Example
3
)
.

The curried ground TRSs for
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{a}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\to\mathsf{f}\circ(\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}),\mathsf{f}\circ(\mathsf{f}\circ(\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}))\to\mathsf{b}\}

For convenience, we will use the abbreviations

\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}=\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a}
,

\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}=\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}
,

\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}=\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}
, and

\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}=\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}

in later examples, so we may write

\mathcal{U}^{\circ}=\{\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{a}\}

and

\mathcal{V}^{\circ}=\{\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a},\mathsf{b}\to\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}\to\mathsf{b}\}
.

3.2.
Flattening

\textbf{flatten}(\mathcal{R}^{\circ})
:

1:

\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\varnothing
,
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\leftarrow\varnothing

2:

for all

\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

do

3:

add
\textbf{mk}^{[]}(\ell)\to\textbf{mk}^{[]}(r)
to
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

—
\mathcal{E}
is modified by
\textbf{mk}^{[]}(\cdot)

\textbf{mk}^{[]}(f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}))
:

1:

for

1\leq i\leq n

do

2:

c_{i}\leftarrow\textbf{mk}^{[]}(s_{i})

—
c_{i}=[s_{i}]

3:

if

f(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\to c\in\mathcal{E}
for some
c

then

4:

return

c

—
c=[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]

5:

else

6:

add
f(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\to c
to
\mathcal{E}
, where
c
is a fresh constant

—
c=[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]

7:

return

c

List of listings 3
Computation of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.

For efficient computations,
it is useful to represent the curried ground TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

using flat rules.
The idea of flattening goes back to Plaisted
[
17
]
.

Definition
\thethm
.

Let a curried TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
be given.
Choose a fresh constant
[s]
for each distinct subterm
s
of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

(
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
),
and let
\Sigma^{[]}=\{[s]\mid s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}
.
The
flattening of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
is given by
(\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{E})
with
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=\{f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]\mid f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\{[\ell]\to[r]\mid\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}

Note in the definition of
\mathcal{E}
,
f
is either a constant
(with
n=0
) or
f={\circ}
(with
n=2
).
Hence

\mathcal{E}
contains a rule
c\to[c]
for each constant subterm
c
of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
and a rule
[s_{1}]\circ[s_{2}]\to[s_{1}\circ s_{2}]
for the remaining subterms

s_{1}\circ s_{2}
of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
The sizes of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
are both

\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)

(which equals
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\|)
.
We can compute the systems
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.
To this end, we may represent
\mathcal{E}

by a lookup table that maps left-hand sides of
\mathcal{E}
to
their corresponding right-hand sides,
and then employ the algorithm from Listing
3
,
using an auxiliary function
\textbf{mk}^{[]}(\cdot)

that maps a ground term
s
to
[s]
while maintaining the
necessary rules in
\mathcal{E}
and creating fresh constants for
the subterms of
s
as necessary.

Example
\thethm

(continued from Example
3.1
)
.

We introduce fresh constants

[\mathsf{a}]
,
[\mathsf{b}]
,
[\mathsf{f}]
,

[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]
,

[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]
,

[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]
, and

[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]
.
The flattening of
\mathcal{U}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\circ}
results
in
(\mathcal{U}^{\flat},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}})
and
(\mathcal{V}^{\flat},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{V}})
, respectively, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\{[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\to[\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\to[\mathsf{b}],[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{a}]\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{a}\to[\mathsf{a}],\mathsf{b}\to[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{f}\to[\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\{[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}],[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{b}],[\mathsf{b}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}],[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]\to[\mathsf{b}]\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}}\cup\{[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{b}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}],[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}],[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}]\}

Both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
are terminating and confluent
(in fact, orthogonal) systems.
Note that for all terms
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
we have
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}[s]
and
[s]\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}s
.
Therefore, we can reconstruct
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
from
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
,
and
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
from
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
as follows:

\mathcal{R}^{\circ}=\{(\ell{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}},r{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}})\mid\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\flat}=\{(\ell{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}},r{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}})\mid\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}

(1)

As a consequence of this observation we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition
\thethm
.

{\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}{\mathrel{\hskip 18.73618pt\hskip-18.73618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-18.73618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.83345pt\hskip-15.83345pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-15.83345pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 14.66685pt\hskip-14.66685pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-14.66685pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}}
.

Proof.

From (
1
),

{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}}

is immediate.
To get the first subset relation,
note that the effect of reducing to normal form with respect to
\mathcal{E}^{-}

is to replace constant subterms
[s]\in\Sigma^{[]}
by
s\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})
;
hence we can compute
C[s]{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
independently on
C
and
s
.
In particular this applies to a rewrite step
C[\ell]\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}C[r]

with
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
.
Together with (
1
) it follows that

{\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}{\mathrel{\hskip 18.73618pt\hskip-18.73618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-18.73618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.83345pt\hskip-15.83345pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-15.83345pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 14.66685pt\hskip-14.66685pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}^{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}$}\hskip-14.66685pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\vphantom{{\mkern-3.0mu!}\hfill}}$}{\leftarrow}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}^{\hfill!\mkern-3.0mu}}}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}}
.
∎

Remark
\thethm
.

Confluence is actually preserved by flattening,
if one replaces
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
by
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

(this will be proved as part of Lemma
7
).
However, this is not the case for the normal form properties,
because the set of normal forms is not preserved.
Verma has given constructions that preserve
UNR
and
UNC
,
but not confluence, in
[
21
,
22
]
.
For ground systems
it is actually possible to preserve confluence and the normal form properties at the same time
if one replaces
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is the subset of
\mathcal{E}^{-}
obtained
by only reversing those rules
[s_{1}]\circ[s_{2}]\to[s_{1}\circ s_{2}]\in\mathcal{E}

for which one of the terms
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
is not a normal form.
The main points are that
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}
will be a normal form with respect
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
if and only if
s
is a normal form with respect to
\mathcal{R}
,
and that
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\prime}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}
if and only if

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
for
s,t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})
.
We do not explore this idea here.

Remark
\thethm
.

An alternative view of the flattening step
that explains some of its utility for decision procedures
beyond restricting the shape of rules
is that it sets up a lookup table (namely,
\mathcal{E}
)
for maximal sharing of the subterms of
\mathcal{R}
.
This is precisely what the
\textbf{mk}^{[]}
function from
Listing
3
does.

3.3.
Recognizing Normal Forms

The set of normal forms of a ground system is a regular language;
in fact this is true for left-linear systems
[
2
]
.
We give a direct construction for ground TRSs.
We assume that we are given a curried ground TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

over a signature
\Sigma
.

With
Q^{\flat}=Q_{f}^{\flat}=\Sigma^{[]}
and
\Delta^{\flat}=\mathcal{E}
,
where
\Sigma^{[]}
and
\mathcal{E}
are obtained by flattening
(Section
3.2
),
we obtain a deterministic tree automaton
that accepts precisely the subterms of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
We modify this automaton to recognize normal forms instead.

Definition
\thethm
.

Let
\star
be a fresh constant
and let

\displaystyle Q={}

\displaystyle Q_{f}=\Sigma^{\mathcal{N}}=\{[s]\mid\text{$s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}$ and $s$ is $\mathcal{R}^{\circ}$-normal form}\}\cup\{[\star]\}

\displaystyle\Delta={}

\displaystyle\{f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]\mid[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]\in Q_{f}-\{[\star]\}\}\cup{}

\displaystyle\{f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to[\star]\mid\text{$f\in\Sigma^{\circ}$, $[s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}]\in Q_{f}$, $f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\not\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}$}\}

The automaton
\mathcal{N}
is given by
(Q,Q_{f},\Delta)
.
Note that due to the restricted signature
\Sigma^{\circ}
,
all transitions have shape

c\to[c]
,
[s_{1}]\circ[s_{2}]\to[s_{1}\circ s_{2}]
, or

[s_{1}]\circ[s_{2}]\to[\star]
,
where in the latter case,
s_{1}=\star
or
s_{2}=\star
may be used.

Example
\thethm

(continued from Example
3.2
)
.

For
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}
, we have
Q=Q_{f}=\{[\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{b}],[\star]\}
and

\Delta
is given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\to[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{b}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\star]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\star]\circ[\mathsf{b}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\star]\circ[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}

\displaystyle\to[\mathsf{b}]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{b}]\circ[\mathsf{b}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{b}]\circ[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{b}]\circ[\star]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\star]\circ[\star]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

For
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
Q=Q_{f}=\{[\mathsf{f}],[\star]\}
and

\Delta
consists of the transitions

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\to[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\star]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\star]\circ[\mathsf{f}]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

\displaystyle[\star]\circ[\star]

\displaystyle\to[\star]

Lemma
\thethm
.

The automaton
\mathcal{N}=(Q,Q_{f},\Delta)
recognizes the set of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms over
\Sigma^{\circ}
.

Proof.

The idea is that the state
[\star]
accepts those
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms

s
that are not subterms of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
while normal form subterms
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
are accepted in state
[s]
.
We show this by induction on
s
.

If
s=f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
is accepted by
\mathcal{N}
in state
q
,
then each
s_{i}
is accepted by
\mathcal{N}
as well,
which means that they are in normal form by the induction hypothesis.
If
q\in\Sigma^{[]}
,
then the last transition being used must be
f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to[s]
,
with
s
in normal form.
Otherwise,
q=[\star]
,
which means that either
s_{i}=\star
for some
i
,
whence
s
has a subterm that is not a subterm of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
or
f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\not\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
In either case,
s
cannot be a left-hand side of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
so it is in normal form.

If
s=f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
is a normal form,
then each
s_{i}
is also a normal form,
and hence accepted by
\mathcal{N}
in a state
q_{i}
by the induction hypothesis.
If any
s_{i}
is accepted in state
[\star]
,
then
f(\dots,\star,\dots)\not\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
ensures
that
f(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})\to[\star]
is a transition of
\mathcal{N}
.
Otherwise
q_{i}=[s_{i}]
for all
i
,
and there is a transition
f(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})\to[s]
or

f(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})\to[\star]
in
\mathcal{N}

depending on whether
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
In either case,
s
is accepted by
\mathcal{N}
as claimed.
∎

In order to compute
\mathcal{N}
efficiently,
let
\overline{Q}_{f}=\{[s]\mid\text{$s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}$ and $s$ is $\mathcal{R}^{\circ}$-reducible}\}
,
which we may compute by the inference rules

\displaystyle[\ell]\in\overline{Q}_{f}[\ell]\to[r]\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\qquad[s_{1}\circ s_{2}]\in\overline{Q}_{f}\lx@proof@logical@and[s_{1}]\circ[s_{2}]\to[s_{1}\circ s_{2}]\in\mathcal{E}[s_{i}]\in\overline{Q}_{f}

where
i\in\{1,2\}
.
The first rule (
side
) expresses that left-hand sides of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
are reducible,
whereas the second rule (
arg
i
) states that
if an argument of a term is reducible,
then the whole term is reducible as well;
because of the restricted signature of the curried system, the root symbol must be
\circ
in this case.
This computation can be done in linear time
(
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)
)
by Horn inference.
Once we have computed
\overline{Q}_{f}
, we can determine
Q=Q_{f}=(\Sigma^{[]}-\overline{Q}_{f})\cup\{[\star]\}
.
For
\Delta
, we can set up a partial function

\delta\colon f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\mapsto q\quad\text{if}\quad f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to q\in\Delta

where
[s_{i}]\in Q_{f}^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq n

that can be queried in logarithmic time, see Listing
4
.

1:

if
there is a rule
f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])\to[s]\in\mathcal{E}

then

2:

if

[s]\in Q_{f}

then

3:

return

[s]

—
normal form subterm of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

4:

else

5:

no result
—
not a normal form

6:

else

7:

return

[\star]

—
normal form, not a subterm of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

List of listings 4
Implementation of
\delta(f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}]))
.

3.4.
Congruence Closure

Congruence closure (introduced by Nelson and Oppen
[
15
]
; a clean and fast implementation can be found in
[
16
]
)
is an efficient method for deciding convertibility of ground terms modulo a set of ground equations; in our case,
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.

The congruence closure procedure consists of two phases.
In the first phase, one determines the congruence classes (hence the name) among the subterms of the given set of equations,
where two subterms
s
and
t
of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
are identified
if and only if they are convertible,
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
.
We write
[s]_{\mathcal{R}}
for the convertibility class
(also known as congruence class) of
s
with respect to
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
The result of the first phase is a system of rewrite rules
\mathcal{C}

that can be expressed concisely as

\mathcal{C}=\{f([s_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[s_{n}]_{\mathcal{R}})\to[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]_{\mathcal{R}}\mid f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}

The key point for efficiency is that it is enough to
consider subterms of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
when computing the congruence closure.
In an implementation,
a representative subterm of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
of
[s]_{\mathcal{R}}
will be stored instead of the whole class.
Let
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}=\{[s]_{\mathcal{R}}\mid s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}\}
,
where we regard the congruence classes
[s]_{\mathcal{R}}
as fresh constants.
The size of
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)
.

Remark
\thethm
.

The earlier notation
[s]
for
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
which suggests a congruence class, can be justified by viewing
[s]
as
the congruence class of
s
with respect to equality.

In the second phase, given two terms
s
and
t
,
one computes the normal forms with respect to rules in
\mathcal{C}
.
The terms
u
and
v
are
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-convertible if and only if
u{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{C}}=v{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{C}}
.
We observe the following.

Proposition
\thethm
.

With the signature
\Sigma^{\circ}\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
,
the set
\mathcal{C}
is an orthogonal, terminating, ground TRS whose rules,
regarded as transitions of a tree automaton, are deterministic.

Proof.

Termination of
\mathcal{C}
is obvious.
Note that if

f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}),f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}

are subterms of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
such that

f([s_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[s_{n}]_{\mathcal{R}})=f([t_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[t_{n}]_{\mathcal{R}})
,
then
s_{i}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Consequently,
f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
,
so
[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]_{\mathcal{R}}=[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})]_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Hence the rules are deterministic as claimed.
∎

Consequently, we may represent
\mathcal{C}
as a deterministic tree automaton

\mathcal{C}=(Q,Q_{f},\Delta)
with
Q=Q_{f}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\Delta=\mathcal{C}
.
Each state
[s]_{\mathcal{R}}
accepts precisely the terms convertible to
s
.
Note that the automaton is not completely defined in general:
Only terms
s
that allow a conversion
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
with a root step are accepted.
1
1

1

The convertibility relation
\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}
is
accepted by the ground tree transducer
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
(cf.
[
2
]
).

The computation of
\mathcal{C}
takes
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time
[
16
]
,
and is based on flattening (Section
3.2
).
As a byproduct of the computation we obtain a map
(\cdot)_{\mathcal{R}}:\Sigma^{[]}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}

with
([s])_{\mathcal{R}}=[s]_{\mathcal{R}}
for
s\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
We lift this map to arbitrary terms over
\Sigma^{\circ}\cup\Sigma^{[]}
by letting

(f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n}))_{\mathcal{R}}=f((s_{1})_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,(s_{n})_{\mathcal{R}})

for
f\in\Sigma^{\circ}
.
By this map,
the key property of the congruence closure that convertible terms
over
\Sigma^{\circ}

are joinable using rules in
\mathcal{C}
can be strengthened
to incorporate flattening, as follows:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For terms
s
and
t
over
\Sigma^{\circ}\cup\Sigma^{[]}
,

(1)

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}t
implies
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
;

(2)

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}t
implies
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
; and

(3)

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
implies
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
.

Consequently,

s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
if and only if

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

(1) We have

f([s_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[s_{n}]_{\mathcal{R}})\to[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{C}

for each

f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{2}])\to[f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})]\in\mathcal{E}
,
so
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}t
implies
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
(2) For
[\ell]\to[r]\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
,

\ell
and
r
are convertible by
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
,
and
[\ell]_{\mathcal{R}}=[r]_{\mathcal{R}}
follows.
Consequently,
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}}t
implies
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
(3) Assume that
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
We show
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
by induction on
s
.
If
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
by a root step,
then
s
and
t
can be written as

s=f([s_{1}],\dots,[s_{n}])
,

t=[t^{\prime}]
,
and there is a subterm
f(s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{n}^{\prime})\trianglelefteq\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
such that

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=f([s_{1}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[s_{n}]_{\mathcal{R}})=f([s_{1}^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}},\dots,[s_{n}^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}[f(s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{n}^{\prime})]_{\mathcal{R}}=[t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}}=(t)_{\mathcal{R}}

Recalling that
[s_{i}]{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}=s_{i}
,
and that
[s_{i}]_{\mathcal{R}}=[s_{i}^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}}
implies
s_{i}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}s_{i}^{\prime}
,
we see that

s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}=f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}f(s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{n}^{\prime})\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t^{\prime}=t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}

If
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
is not a root step, then either

s=[s^{\prime}]
,
t=[t^{\prime}]
with
[s^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}}=[t^{\prime}]_{\mathcal{R}}
,
and
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}=s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t^{\prime}=t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
follows,
or we can write
s=f(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
,
t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})

such that
(s_{i})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{=}}(t_{i})_{\mathcal{R}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
We conclude by the induction hypothesis.

For the final claim, assume that
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
.
Let
s^{\prime}=s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
and note that
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}s
,
and that
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})
.
Therefore,
s^{\prime}=(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}(s)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
Hence
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
implies

s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}

by Proposition
3.2
,

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}

by the previous observation and the fact that
s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
and
t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
are terms over
\Sigma^{\circ}
,
and
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
by confluence of
\mathcal{C}
.
Conversely,
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}

implies

s\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}^{*}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}t

by part (3)
and

s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t

by Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Example
\thethm

(continued from Example
3.2
)
.

For
\mathcal{U}^{\circ}
,
restricted to the subterms of the system,
there are two congruence classes,

[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}}=\{\mathsf{f}\}
and

[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}\}
.
We have

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{U}}=\{\mathsf{f}\to[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}},\mathsf{a}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}},\mathsf{b}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}},[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}}\circ[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}}\}

For
\mathcal{V}^{\circ}
,
restricted to the subterms of the system,
the congruence classes are
[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{V}}=\{\mathsf{f}\}
and

[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{V}}=\{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}\}
,
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}
is essentially the same as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{U}}
:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}=\{\mathsf{f}\to[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{V}},\mathsf{a}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{V}},\mathsf{b}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{V}},[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{V}}\circ[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{V}}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{V}}\}

3.5.
Rewrite Closure

The rewrite closure is based on the flattened view

(\mathcal{R}^{\flat},\mathcal{E})
of the curried TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
(Section
3.2
).
In the following,
p
and
q
range over
\Sigma^{[]}
.

Definition
\thethm
.

The
rewrite closure of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
is given by

\textstyle\mathcal{F}=\{p\to q\mid p,q\in\Sigma^{[]}\text{ and }p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}q\}

Note that whenever
p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
,
(p)_{\mathcal{R}}=(q)_{\mathcal{R}}
;
consequently, the map
(\cdot)_{\mathcal{R}}
is invariant under rewriting by
\mathcal{F}
.

Proposition
\thethm
.

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}t
implies
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=(t)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
∎

The point of the rewrite closure is that reachability in
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

can be decomposed into a
decreasing
sequence of steps in
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
,
followed by an
increasing
sequence of steps in
\mathcal{E}^{-}
and
\mathcal{F}
.
Formally, we have the following lemma.

Lemma
\thethm
.

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
if and only if

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t
.

Proof.

Consider Definition
3.5
.
Since rules of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}
have elements of
\Sigma^{[]}
on both sides,
we have
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.
We also have
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}}
.
Therefore, the reachability relation is preserved by the rewrite closure,
i.e.,

{\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}}={\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}}
.

Now consider a reduction
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
.
Then
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t
,
because of
\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.
Assume that this rewrite
sequence is not of the shape

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t
,
but has a minimal number of inversions between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
steps
among the reductions
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t

(an inversion is any pair of an
\mathcal{E}
step following an
\mathcal{E}^{-}
step,
not necessarily directly).
Then there is a subsequence of shape

s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[p,\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}s^{\prime\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t^{\prime\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[q,\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}t^{\prime}
, starting
with an
\mathcal{E}^{-}
step at position
p
and a final
\mathcal{E}
step at position
q
.
The cases
p<q
or
p>q
are impossible,
because the rules of
\mathcal{F}
only affect constants from
\Sigma^{[]}
,
and all rules from
\mathcal{E}
have a function symbol from
\Sigma^{\circ}

at the root of their left-hand side, with constants from
\Sigma^{[]}

as arguments.

If
p=q
then

s^{\prime}|_{p}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t^{\prime}|_{p}
, which implies

s^{\prime}|_{p}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t^{\prime}|_{p}
.
We also have
s^{\prime}|_{p},t^{\prime}|_{p}\in\Sigma^{[]}
because these two terms
are right-hand sides of rules in
\mathcal{E}
.
Consequently,
s^{\prime}|_{p}\to s^{\prime}|_{p}\in\mathcal{F}
follows by Definition
3.5
.
Hence we can delete the two
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
steps and the
\mathcal{F}
steps
of
s^{\prime}|_{p}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t^{\prime}|_{p}
,
and replace them by a single
\mathcal{F}
step using the rule
s^{\prime}|_{p}\to t^{\prime}|_{p}
.
This decreases the number of inversions between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
steps,
contradicting our minimality assumption.
Finally, if
p\parallel q
, then we
can reorder the rewrite sequence
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t^{\prime}
as

s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[>q,\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{>q,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{>q,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{>q,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[q,\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{q,\mathcal{E}}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[p,\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{p,\mathcal{E}^{-}}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[>p,\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{>p,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{>p,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{>p,\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t^{\prime}
,
commuting mutually parallel rewrite steps.
This reduces the number of inversions between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
steps,
and again we reach a contradiction.
∎

The definition of
\mathcal{F}
does not lend itself to an effective computation.
Lemma
3.5
shows that we could alternatively define

\mathcal{F}={\leadsto}
,
where
\leadsto
is determined by the inference rules in Figure
5
.
Note that the congruence rule (
cong
) is specialized to the binary symbol
\circ
.
This is because all other elements
c\in\Sigma^{\circ}
are constants,
and the congruence arising from
c\to[c]\in\mathcal{E}

is an instance of (
refl
) using
p=[c]
.

Lemma
\thethm
.

We have
p\leadsto q
if and only if
p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.5
,

p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
is equivalent to
p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
.
All the inference rules in Figure
5
are consistent with
the requirement that
p\leadsto q
implies
p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
.
The most interesting case is the (
cong
) rule,
for which the following reduction from
p
to
q
is obtained:

p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}q

We show that
p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
implies
p\leadsto q
by contradiction.
Assume that
p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
but not
p\leadsto q
.
Let
p=t_{0}\to\dots\to t_{n}=q
be a shortest sequence of

(\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F})
steps from
p
to
q
, and pick
p
and
q

such that
n
is minimal. If
n=0
then
p=q
,
and
p\leadsto q
by (
refl
).
If
n=1
then
p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
since
\mathcal{E}
-rules
are rooted by elements of
\Sigma^{\circ}
on their left-hand sides.
If
t_{i}\in\Sigma^{[]}
for any
0<i<n
, then

p\leadsto t_{i}\leadsto q
by minimality of

p\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
, and
p\leadsto q
by transitivity
(
trans
).
If any
t_{i}=c\in\Sigma
then
we must have
t_{i-1}=[c]\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}t_{i}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}[c]=t_{i+1}
,
and removing these two rewrite steps from the sequence
results in a shorter reduction from
p
to
q
, contradicting minimality.
In the remaining case, we have
t_{i}=p_{i}\circ q_{i}
for all

0<i<n
, and hence
p_{1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}p_{n-1}

and
q_{1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q_{n-1}
since any root step
would have a constant from
\Sigma^{[]}
as source or target. But
these two rewrite sequences have length at most
n-2
,
and therefore
p_{1}\leadsto p_{n-1}
and
q_{1}\leadsto q_{n-1}
hold.
This implies
p\leadsto q
by the (
cong
) rule.
In all cases we found that
p\leadsto q
, a contradiction.
∎

\displaystyle p\leadsto pp\in\Sigma^{[]}\qquad p\leadsto qp\to q\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}\qquad p\leadsto r\lx@proof@logical@and p,q,r\in\Sigma^{[]}p\leadsto qq\leadsto r

\displaystyle p\leadsto q\lx@proof@logical@and p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p\in\mathcal{E}p_{1}\leadsto q_{1}p_{2}\leadsto q_{2}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{E}

Inference rules for rewrite closure

\textbf{rewrite-closure}(n,\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}^{\flat})
: compute rewrite closure
\mathcal{F}={\leadsto}

0:

\Sigma^{[]}=\{1,\dots,n\}
, which can be achieved as part of the flattening step

1:

(\leadsto)\leftarrow\varnothing\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}^{2}
(represented by an array).

2:

for

1\leq p\leq n

do

3:

\textbf{add}(p,p)

—
(refl)

4:

for all

p\to q\in\mathcal{R}^{\flat}

do

5:

\textbf{add}(p,q)

—
(base)

\textbf{add}(p,q)
: add
p\leadsto q
and process implied (
trans
) and (
cong
) rules

1:

if

p\leadsto q

then

2:

return

3:

extend
\leadsto
by
p\leadsto q

4:

for

1\leq r\leq n

do

5:

if

r\leadsto p

then

6:

\textbf{add}(r,q)

—
(trans)

7:

if

q\leadsto r

then

8:

\textbf{add}(p,r)

—
(trans)

9:

for all

p\circ p_{2}\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
and
q\circ q_{2}\to q_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
with
p_{2}\leadsto q_{2}

do

10:

\textbf{add}(p_{r},q_{r})

—
(cong)

11:

for all

p_{1}\circ p\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
and
q_{1}\circ q\to q_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
with
p_{1}\leadsto q_{1}

do

12:

\textbf{add}(p_{r},q_{r})

—
(cong)

Algorithm for rewrite closure

Example
\thethm

(continued from Example
3.2
)
.

We present
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{V}}
as tables, where non-empty
entries correspond to the rules contained in each TRS,
and we leave out the surrounding brackets for the elements of
\Sigma^{[]}
.
For example,
[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\notin\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}

but
[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
The letters indicate the inference rule used to derive the entry, while
the superscripts indicate stage numbers—each inference uses only
premises that have smaller stage numbers.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}=\begin{array}[]{r|cccc}&\mathsf{f}&\mathsf{a}&\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}&\mathsf{b}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{f}&r^{0}&&&\\
\mathsf{a}&&r^{0}&&\\
\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}&&b^{0}&r^{0}&b^{0}\\
\mathsf{b}&&&&r^{0}\\
\end{array}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{V}}=\begin{array}[]{r|cccccccc}&\mathsf{f}&\mathsf{a}&\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}&\mathsf{b}&\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}&\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}&\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{f}&r^{0}&&&&&&\\
\mathsf{a}&&r^{0}&b^{0}&b^{0}&t^{2}&t^{1}&t^{3}\\
\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}&&&r^{0}&t^{3}&c^{1}&t^{3}&t^{2}\\
\mathsf{b}&&&&r^{0}&t^{4}&b^{0}&t^{4}\\
\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}&&&&t^{2}&r^{0}&t^{2}&c^{1}\\
\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}&&&&t^{4}&c^{3}&r^{0}&c^{3}\\
\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}f{\mkern-1.5mu}b}&&&&b^{0}&t^{4}&t^{1}&r^{0}\\
\end{array}

The size of
\leadsto
(and hence
\mathcal{F}
) is bounded by
|\Sigma^{[]}|^{2}\in\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2})
.
We can view the inference rules in Figure
5
as a system
of Horn clauses with atoms of the form
p\leadsto q
(
p,q\in\Sigma^{[]}
).
This system can be solved in time proportional to the total size of the
clauses, finding a minimal solution for
the set
\mathcal{F}
.
There are

|\Sigma^{[]}|
instances of (
refl
),

|\mathcal{R}^{\flat}|=|\mathcal{R}|
instances of (
base
),

|\Sigma^{[]}|^{3}
instances of (
trans
)
and at most
|\Sigma^{[]}|^{2}
instances of (
cong
),
noting that
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
are determined by
p

and
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
are determined by
q
.
Therefore, we can compute
\mathcal{F}
in time
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
.

Remark
\thethm
.

In our implementation, we do not generate these Horn clauses explicitly.
Instead, whenever we make a new inference
p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
, we check all
possible rules that involve
p\to q\in\mathcal{F}
as a premise. The result
is an incremental algorithm (see Listing
6
).
From an abstract point of view, however,
this is essentially the same as solving the Horn clauses as stated above.
This remark also applies to inference rules presented later.

4.
Deciding
UNC

In this section we develop an algorithm that decides
UNC
for a finite ground TRS
\mathcal{R}

in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time for a given finite ground TRS
\mathcal{R}
.
As preprocessing steps, we curry the TRS to bound its arity
while preserving and reflecting
UNC
(Section
3.1
),
compute the automaton
\mathcal{N}
that accepts the
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
(Section
3.3
),
and the congruence closure
\mathcal{C}

that allows for an efficient checking of convertibility (Section
3.4
).

First note that if we have two distinct convertible normal forms
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
such that the conversion does not contain a root step,
then there are strict subterms of
s
and
t
that are convertible and distinct.
Therefore,
UNC
reduces to the question whether any state of
\mathcal{C}
,
the automaton produced by the congruence closure of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
(cf. Section
3.4
),
accepts more than one normal form.
Let
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}
be the result of the product construction on
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{N}
,
where
\mathcal{N}
is the automaton accepting the
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms (cf. Section
3.3
).
We can decide
UNC
by enumerating accepting runs
t\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
in a bottom-up fashion (see Figure
7
) until either

•

we obtain two distinct accepting runs ending in
(p_{1},q_{1})
and
(p_{2},q_{2})
with
p_{1}=p_{2}
(which means that the two corresponding terms are convertible normal forms),
in which case
\text{UNC}(\mathcal{R})
does not hold; or

•

we have exhausted all runs,
in which case
\text{UNC}(\mathcal{R})
holds.

This enumeration of accepting runs is performed by the algorithm in Listing
8
.
Note that for achieving the desired complexity it is crucial that
on lines
10
and
12
,
rather than iterating over all transitions of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{N}
,
appropriate indices are used;
for line
12
, this is the partial function
\delta
from Section
3.3
,
while for line
10
,
one can precompute an array that maps each state
p
of
\mathcal{C}
to
a list of transitions from
\mathcal{C}

where
p
occurs on the left-hand side.

Example
\thethm

(continued from Examples
3.3
and
3.4
)
.

Consider
\mathcal{U}
. The constant normal forms are
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{b}
,
so we add
([\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}},[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
and
([\mathsf{b}]_{\mathcal{U}}=[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}},[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})

to the
worklist
. The main loop (lines
5
–
13
),
is entered three times.

(1)

([\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}},[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
is taken from the

,
and we assign
\textit{seen}([\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}})=([\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
.
There are no transitions in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{U}}
satisfying the conditions in lines

and

.

(2)

([\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}},[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
is taken from the
