,
and we assign
\textit{seen}([\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}})=([\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
.
This time, the transitions

[\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{U}}\circ[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}}\to[\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{U}}

and
[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{b}]\to[\star]\in\mathcal{N}
satisfy the

,

.

(3)

.
Since
\textit{seen}([\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}})

we conclude that
\text{UNC}(\mathcal{U})
does not hold.
Indeed
\mathsf{b}

(from
\textit{seen}([\mathsf{a}]_{\mathcal{U}})=([\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
)
and
\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}
are convertible normal forms for
\mathcal{U}^{\circ}
:

\mathsf{b}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{}}{\mathrel{\quad\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 7.97224pt\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 6.80565pt\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{}}{\mathrel{\quad\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 7.97224pt\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 6.80565pt\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}\mathsf{f}\circ(\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}
.

For
\mathcal{V}
, there is only one constant normal form, namely
\mathsf{f}
.
Hence, initially, we add
([\mathsf{f}]_{\mathcal{V}},[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
to the

.

–

).
On line

,

.
At this point,

could succeed
would be having a transition with left-hand side

in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{V}}
, but there is no such a transition.
Hence the main loop terminates and we conclude that
\text{UNC}(\mathcal{V})
is true.

List of listings 8
Deciding
\text{UNC}(\mathcal{R})

Correctness of the procedure hinges on two key facts:
First, the automaton
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}
is deterministic,
which means that distinct runs result from distinct terms.
Secondly, the set of
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms is closed under taking subterms,
so we can skip non-normal forms in the enumeration.

Lemma
\thethm
.

Whenever the algorithm in Listing
8
reaches line
5
,
the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

seen(p)=(q,s)
or
(p,q,s)\in\mathit{worklist}
implies a run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
;

(2)

the elements of
\mathit{worklist}
are distinct,
and

seen(p)=(q,s)
implies
(p,q,s)\notin\mathit{worklist}
; and

(3)

for any run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
, either

seen(p)=(q,s)
, or
there is a subterm
s^{\prime}\trianglelefteq s

and a run
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

such that
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathit{worklist}
.

Proof.

For the first property,
note that only lines
4
and
13
extend the
worklist
.
On line
4
, we have
c\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}([c]_{\mathcal{R}},[c])
by (
const
)
from Figure
7
.
On line
13
,
we have
s_{1}\circ s_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p_{r},q_{r})

by (
app
).
Further note that when we update
\textit{seen}(p)
on line
9
,

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
holds because
(p,q,s)
was previously on the
\mathit{worklist}
.

For the second property, first note that
s
determines the values of
p
and
q
in all relevant triples
(p,q,s)
,
so we can focus on the terms produced in lines
4
and
13
.
Line
4
produces all possible constants for
s
, each exactly once.
As long as the procedure doesn’t terminate,
any term removed from the
worklist
on line
6
ends up in the
\mathit{seen}
map on line
9
,
so as long as the invariant is maintained, the terms
s
obtained on line
6
will be distinct.
Now a term
s_{1}\circ s_{2}
will only be produced on line
13
when
the term
s
just taken from the
worklist
equals
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
,
and furthermore, either
s_{1}=s_{2}
, or the other term had previously
been taken from the
worklist
.
Clearly this can happen only once, establishing the invariant.

For the third invariant,
let us say that the invariant holds for
s
if
there is a run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
and the
third invariant holds for that run.
First note that every run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
must use (
const
)
at the leafs,
so any constant subterm
c\trianglelefteq s
will
satisfy the invariant after the initialization on lines
1
–
4

is completed.
We show by induction on
s

that the invariant for
s
is maintained in the main loop.
Note that once
\mathit{seen}(p)=(q,s)
,
that will remain true because line
9
never updates a defined value of
\mathit{seen}
.
Furthermore, if
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}
,
then the invariant will be maintained until
(p,q,s)
is taken from the
worklist
on line
6
,
but then the invariant will be restored on line
9
.
In particular, the invariant is maintained for constants
s
.
So assume that
s
is not constant, i.e.,
s=s_{1}\circ s_{2}
,
and that neither
\textit{seen}(p)=(q,s)
nor
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}
.
We can decompose the run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
as

s_{1}\circ s_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p_{1},q_{1})\circ(p_{2},q_{2})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}}(p,q)
.
At the end of the loop body (line
13
),
the invariant will hold for
s_{1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p_{1},q_{1})
and

s_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p_{2},q_{2})
.
This ensures the invariant for
s
except when

\textit{seen}(p_{1})=(q_{1},s_{1})
and
\textit{seen}(p_{2})=(q_{2},s_{2})
.
But in that case,
because the invariant held for
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
at the beginning of the loop iteration,
one of
\textit{seen}(p_{1})
or
\textit{seen}(p_{2})
must just have been assigned,
i.e., either
(p_{1},q_{1},s_{1})
or
(p_{2},q_{2},s_{2})
must have been taken from the
worklist
on line
6
.
Hence the transitions
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p\in\mathcal{C}
and
q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{N}
satisfy the conditions on lines
11
–
13

and
(p,q,s)
is added to the
worklist
on line
13
,
restoring the invariant for
s
.
∎

Theorem
\thethm
.

The algorithm in Listing
8
is correct and runs in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.

Proof.

The first two invariants established in Lemma
4
ensure
that if
\mathit{seen}(p)=(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
on line
8
,
then we have distinct runs
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)

and
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q^{\prime})
witnessing
that
s
and
s^{\prime}
are distinct convertible normal forms, so
UNC
does not hold.
On the other hand, if line
14
is reached,
then by the third invariant,
we have
seen(p)=(q,s)

for all runs
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{N}}^{*}}(p,q)
,
showing that any normal form in the convertibility class represented by
p

equals
s
, which establishes
UNC
.
Termination follows from the complexity bound.

So let us focus on the complexity.
Let
n=\|\mathcal{R}\|
.
As stated in Section
3.4
,
computing
\mathcal{C}
takes
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
time.
In Section
3.3
we presented a
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
time computation
for
\mathcal{N}
where the transitions are represented
by a partial function
\delta
that can be queried in
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
time
per left-hand side.
This covers line
1
of the algorithm.
Lines
3
and
4
take
\mathcal{O}(n)
time
(the normal form constants are already enumerated in the computation of
\mathcal{N}
).
Observe that lines
9
–
13
update
\mathit{seen}
,
so they are executed at most once per element of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}(n)
times.
The precomputation for line
10
takes
\mathcal{O}(n)
time as well.
In line
10
, each transition
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{C}

is encountered at most twice, once for
p_{1}

and a second time for
p_{2}
,
which means that lines
11
–
13

are executed at most twice per transition in
\mathcal{C}
, so
\mathcal{O}(n)
times.
The check on line
12
takes
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
time per iteration, so
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
time in total.
Finally, we note that line
13
is executed
\mathcal{O}(n)
times, so no more than
\mathcal{O}(n)
items are ever added to the
worklist
,
which means that lines
5
–
8
are executed
\mathcal{O}(n)
times.
Overall the algorithm executes in
\mathcal{O}(n\log n)
time, as claimed.
∎

Remark
\thethm
.

The third component of the elements of
\mathit{worklist}

as well as the second component of the results of
\mathit{seen}

in Listing
8

were added to aid the proof of Theorem
4

and can be omitted in an actual implementation.

5.
Deciding
UNR

In this section we present a procedure that decides
UNR
for a finite ground TRS
\mathcal{R}

in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3}\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.
As preprocessing, we curry the TRS to bound its arity
while preserving and reflecting
UNR
(Section
3.1
),
compute the automaton
\mathcal{N}
that accepts the
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
(Section
3.3
),
and the rewrite closure
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
that
allows checking reachability (Section
3.5
).

5.1.
Peak Analysis

In order to derive conditions for
UNR
,
assume that
UNR
does not hold and consider
a peak
s\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{!}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.48618pt\hskip-20.48618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}^{!}$}\hskip-20.48618pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 17.50014pt\hskip-17.50014pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}^{!}$}\hskip-17.50014pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.33354pt\hskip-16.33354pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}}^{!}$}\hskip-16.33354pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{\vphantom{!}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{!}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{!}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{!}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{!}}t

between distinct
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
s
and
t

of minimum total size.
If the peak has no root step then we can project it to the arguments,
and obtain a smaller counterexample.
So, without loss of generality,
we may assume that the peak has a root step in its left part.
Using the rewrite closure (cf. Lemma
3.5
),
the peak can be decomposed as

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t

where, in fact, the left part has a root step.
This means that there is a constant
p
such that

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t

(2)

First we consider the special case

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
,
which implies that any
p
is reachable from at most one normal form
using rules from
\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}
.

Definition
\thethm
.

The
first
UNR
-condition
holds if

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t

for
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
s,t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})

and
p\in\Sigma^{[]}

implies
s=t
.

If the first
UNR
-condition is violated, then
UNR
clearly does not hold.
Assume that the first
UNR
-condition is satisfied and
let
w
be the partial function that witnesses this fact:

w(p)=t\iff\text{$t$ is a normal form and $\textstyle t\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p$}

In order to analyze the conversion (
2
) further,
note that every forward
\mathcal{E}
step between
p
and
t
is preceded
by an inverse
\mathcal{E}
step at the same position,
inducing a peak between two constants from
\Sigma^{[]}
.
This situation is captured by the following definition.

Definition
\thethm
.

Two constants
p,q\in\Sigma^{[]}
are
meetable

if
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
.
In this case we write
p\uparrow q
.

Using
\uparrow^{\mnshortparallel}
,
the parallel closure of the relation
\uparrow
,
we can find a multi-hole context
C
and
constants
q_{1},\dots,q_{n}
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}

such that (
2
) becomes

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}q\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}C[q_{1},\dots,q_{n}]\uparrow^{\mnshortparallel}C[p_{1},\dots,p_{n}]\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t

(3)

Note that because
s
and
t
are normal forms,
we have
s=w(q)
and
t=C[w(p_{1}),\dots,w(p_{n})]
.

Definition
\thethm
.

The
second
UNR
-condition
holds if
(
3
) for
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
s,t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})

implies
s=t
.

The analysis of this section is summarized by the following lemma.

Lemma
\thethm
.

A curried ground TRS is
UNR
if and only if the first and second

UNR
-conditions are satisfied.
∎

5.2.
Computing Meetable Constants

The meetable constant relation
\uparrow
can be computed in a
way similar to the rewrite closure from Section
3.5
,
using the inference rules in Figure
9
.
So for this subsection, let
\uparrow
be defined by those inference rules.
The following lemma shows that

\uparrow
coincides with the meetable constants relation,
justifying the symbol.

Lemma
\thethm
.

For
p,q\in\Sigma^{[]}
,

p\uparrow q

if and only if
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same principles as that of Lemma
3.5
,
so let us be brief.
First note that all rules in Figure
9
are consistent
with the requirement that
p\uparrow q
implies
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
.

On the other hand,
assume that there is peak

p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q

such that
p\uparrow q
does not hold.
Choose such a peak of minimal length.
Then either
p=q
, and (
refl
) applies,
or
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.65976pt\hskip-15.65976pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-15.65976pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.7987pt\hskip-12.7987pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-12.7987pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.63211pt\hskip-11.63211pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-11.63211pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}p^{\prime}\uparrow q
and (
step
l
) applies,
or
p\uparrow q^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}q
and (
step
r
) applies,
or
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}\uparrow^{\mnshortparallel}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}q
,
in which case (
cong
) applies.
In each case,
p\uparrow q
follows, contradicting the assumption.
∎

\displaystyle p\uparrow p\mathstrut p\in\Sigma^{[]}\qquad p\uparrow q\lx@proof@logical@and p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p\in\mathcal{E}p_{1}\uparrow q_{1}p_{2}\uparrow q_{2}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle p\uparrow r\lx@proof@logical@and q\to p\in\mathcal{F}q\uparrow rr\in\Sigma^{[]}\qquad p\uparrow r\lx@proof@logical@and p\in\Sigma^{[]}p\uparrow qq\to r\in\mathcal{F}

Figure 9.
Inference rules for meetable constants

Note that as in the case of the rewrite closure,
there is no (
cong
) rule for constants
c\in\Sigma^{\circ}
,
because they would be instances of (

).
There are
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)

),
There are
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2})

)
(because

and

and
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})

step

)
and (
step

).
Using Horn inference with
p\uparrow q
as atoms,
the computation of
\uparrow
takes
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
time.

Checking the first

-condition and computing

We start by checking the first
UNR
-condition.
To perform this computation efficiently,
we make use of the automaton
\mathcal{N}
that recognizes normal forms,
cf. Section
3.3
.
The fact that
s
is a
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form is witnessed by a run
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}q
.
Let

\textstyle W(p,q)=\{s\mid s\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})\text{, }s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p\in\Sigma^{[]}\text{ and }s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}q\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{N}}\}

Lemma
\thethm
.

The predicate
s\in W(p,q)
is characterized by the inference rules in Figure
10
.

Proof.

The inference rules follow by an inductive analysis of the last step
of the
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p
reduction,
where
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}q
.
Recall that
\mathcal{N}
is deterministic, so
q
is determined by
s
.

(
base
)

If there is a single step, then it must be using a rule
s=c\to[c]=p
from
\mathcal{E}
,
where
c\in\Sigma
, and
c
is a
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form,
which ensures that
c\to[c]=q\in\mathcal{N}
as well.

(
step
F
)

If the last step is an
\mathcal{F}^{-}
step,
then
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}p
,
and there is a
q
with
s\in W(p,q)
.

(
step
E
)

If the last step is an
\mathcal{E}
step but
s
is not a constant,
then
s=s_{1}\circ s_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}p
,
and there are
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
with
s_{1}\in W(p_{1},q_{1})
and
s_{2}\in W(p_{2},q_{2})
.

Conversely, each derivation of
s\in W(p,q)
by these three
inference rules gives rise to rewrite sequences

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p
and
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{N}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{N}}^{*}}q
.
∎

The corresponding code is given in Listing
11
.
In addition to
w(q)
(which we introduced immediately after
Definition
5.1
)
we also compute a partial function
n(q)

which returns the state of
\mathcal{N}
that accepts
w(q)

if the latter is defined.
The computed witnesses may have exponential size
(see Example
5.3
),
so in order to make the check on line
8
efficient,
it is crucial to use maximal sharing.

Example
\thethm
.

We exhibit a class of TRSs with exponential witness size.
To this end, fix
k>0
and consider the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{b}

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{a}_{i-1}\circ\mathsf{a}_{i-1}

where
0<i\leq k
.
The check of the first
UNR
-condition
will find the two normal forms
\mathsf{b}
and
t_{k}
of
\mathsf{a}_{k}
,
where
t_{0}=\mathsf{a}_{0}
and
t_{i+1}=t_{i}\circ t_{i}
for
0<i\leq k
.
The term
t_{k}
has size
2^{k+1}-1
,
but only
k-1
distinct subterms.

Remark
\thethm
.

The check of the first
UNR
-condition (Listing
11
)
is similar to the check of
UNC
(Listing
8
),
with a few crucial differences:

•

First, we use the automaton
\mathcal{A}=(\Sigma^{[]},\Sigma^{[]},\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-})

instead of
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Because
\mathcal{A}
is not deterministic
(the rules of
\mathcal{F}^{-}
are
\epsilon
-transitions),
different runs may result in the same term.
Hence the check on line
8
is needed,
and witnesses need to be stored,
using maximal sharing for efficient equality tests.

•

Furthermore, in addition to lines
10
–
13
in Listing
8
,
which correspond to lines
12
–
15
in Listing
11
,
we need a similar loop processing the
\epsilon
-transitions from
\mathcal{F}^{-}
,
cf. lines
16
–
17
in Listing
11
.
The latter change increases the complexity from
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
to
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2})
.

\mathcal{F}

p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.65976pt\hskip-15.65976pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-15.65976pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.7987pt\hskip-12.7987pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-12.7987pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.63211pt\hskip-11.63211pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{F}}}$}\hskip-11.63211pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{F}}$}{\leftarrow}}}p^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 26.42368pt\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-26.42368pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.02106pt\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.02106pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 20.85446pt\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}}^{*}$}\hskip-20.85446pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}C[q_{1},\dots,q_{n}]
,
and
t\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
for some
q
;
in that case,
t\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q)
follows.

(
step
{}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
)

If the leftmost step is an
\mathcal{E}
step,
then either
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t\in\Sigma
,
but that case is already covered by (
base
′
),
or
p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\mathrel{\hskip 15.87503pt\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-15.87503pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.9723pt\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-12.9723pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 11.80571pt\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}}$}\hskip-11.80571pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}}$}{\leftarrow}}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}
,
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
,
and there are states
q_{1}
,
q_{2}

with
t_{1}\in W^{\prime}(p_{1},q_{1})
,
t_{2}\in W^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})
,
and a transition
q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q_{r}\in\mathcal{N}
.
∎

Because
W^{\prime}(p,q)
may be an infinite set,
we instead compute the partial function
w^{\prime}(p,q)
that returns

s
if
W^{\prime}(p,q)=\{s\}
is a singleton set,
or the special value
\infty
if
W^{\prime}(p,q)
has at least two elements,
where
\infty
is distinct from any term and satisfies

\infty\circ t=t\circ\infty=\infty\circ\infty=\infty

for all terms
t
.
The system has the
UNR
property if
w^{\prime}(p,q)=w(p)
whenever
w^{\prime}(p,q)
and
w(p)
are both defined.

The procedure is given in Listing
13
.
It maintains a
worklist
of tuples
(p,q,s)
where

s
is either a term with
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
,
or has the special value
s=\infty
.
Note that each value
w^{\prime}(p,q)
may be updated up to two times:
it starts out as undefined, may be updated to an element of
W^{\prime}(p,q)
,
and later to
\infty
if
W^{\prime}(p,q)
has at least two elements.
These updates are performed by lines
7
–
12
.

Example
\thethm

(continued from Examples
5.2
and
5.3
)
.

Let us check the second
UNR
-condition for

\mathcal{U}^{\circ}=\{\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}\to\mathsf{a},\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}\to\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{a}\}

according to Listing
13
.
On line
4
, we put the following items on the
worklist
:

•

([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
,
because
w([\mathsf{f}])=\mathsf{f}
and
[\mathsf{f}]\uparrow[\mathsf{f}]
;

•

([\mathsf{b}],[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
,
because
w([\mathsf{b}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{b}]\uparrow[\mathsf{b}]
,
and
w([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{b}]\uparrow[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]
;

•

([\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
,
because
w([\mathsf{b}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{a}]\uparrow[\mathsf{b}]
,
and
w([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{a}]\uparrow[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]
; and

•

([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}],[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
,
because
w([\mathsf{b}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\uparrow[\mathsf{b}]
,
and
w([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}])=\mathsf{b}
and
[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\uparrow[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]

Ignoring duplicates on the
worklist

(which are skipped on line
9
),
the main loop (lines
5
–
20
) is executed 3 times.

(1)

([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
is taken from the
worklist
,
and we let
w^{\prime}([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}])=\mathsf{f}
.

([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
is added to the
worklist
on line
20
.

(2)

([\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
is taken from the
worklist
,
and we let
w^{\prime}([\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{b}])=\mathsf{b}
.
Now line
18
is reached for

[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}]\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{U}}
,

w^{\prime}([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}])=\mathsf{f}
,
w^{\prime}([\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{b}])=\mathsf{b}
,
and
[\mathsf{f}]\circ[\mathsf{b}]\to[\star]\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}
.
Hence
([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}],[\star],\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b})
is added to the
worklist
.
Furthermore,
([\mathsf{a}],[\mathsf{b}],\mathsf{b})
is added to the
worklist

on line
20
because
[\mathsf{a}]\to[\mathsf{a}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}
.

(3)

([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}],[\star],\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b})
is taken from the
worklist
.
We set
w^{\prime}([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}],[\star])=\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}
.
The check on line
13
succeeds
(
w([\mathsf{f{\mkern-1.5mu}a}])=\mathsf{b}\neq\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}
),
so the second
UNR
-condition is not satisfied.

We conclude that
\text{UNR}(\mathcal{U})
does not hold,
a fact witnessed by

\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{b}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{}}{\mathrel{\quad\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-10.00002pt\hbox{$\textstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 7.97224pt\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-7.97224pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 6.80565pt\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}\hskip-6.80565pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vphantom{\leftarrow}$}{\leftarrow}}}\mathsf{f}\circ(\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{f}\circ\mathsf{a}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{}}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathsf{b}
.

For
\mathcal{V}
,
[\mathsf{f}]\uparrow[\mathsf{f}]
is the only meetable constant relation
with defined witness
w(q)
,
so
([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}],\mathsf{f})
is the only item ever pushed to the
worklist

(twice, because
[\mathsf{f}]\to[\mathsf{f}]\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{V}}
),
corresponding to
\mathsf{f}\in W^{\prime}([\mathsf{f}],[\mathsf{f}])
.
The second
UNR
-condition holds for
\mathcal{V}
,
implying
\text{UNR}(\mathcal{V})
.

In order to achieve the desired complexity,
care must be taken with the enumeration on line
15
:
instead of iterating over all elements of
\mathcal{E}
,
an index mapping
p
to the rules
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{E}

with
p\in\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
should be used.

The following two lemmas establish key invariants for showing
that the procedures in Listings
11
and
13

faithfully implement the inference rules in Figures
10
and
12
,
respectively.

Lemma
\thethm
.

Whenever line
5
is reached in Listing
11
, we have

(1)

If
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}
or
s=w(p)
is defined and
q=n(p)
,
then
s\in W(p,q)
holds.

(2)

Assume that
\hat{s}\in W(\hat{p},\hat{q})
can be inferred using
an inference rule from Figure
10

with premises
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
.
Then either
(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})\in\textit{worklist}
,
or
\hat{s}=w(\hat{p})
and
\hat{q}=n(\hat{p})
,
or there is a premise
P_{i}=s^{\prime}\in W(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

such that
w(p^{\prime})
is undefined or
s^{\prime}\neq w(p^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For the first invariant,
first note that
w(p)
and
n(p)
are updated simultaneously on line
11

with values that are taken from the
worklist
on line
6
,
so we may focus on the addition of items to the
worklist
,
which happens on lines
4
,
15
and
17
.
On line
4
,
c
is a normal form,
and
c\in W([c],[c])
holds by rule (
base
).
On line
15
, we have
s_{1}\in W(p_{1},q_{1})
and
s_{2}\in W(p_{2},q_{2})
,
and rules
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
and
q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q_{r}\in\mathcal{N}
,
allowing us to infer
s_{1}\circ s_{2}\in W(p_{r},q_{r})
by (
step
E
).
Similarly, on line
17
,
we have
s\in W(p,q)
and
p^{\prime}\to p\in\mathcal{F}
,
and
s\in W(p^{\prime},q)
follows by (
step
F
).

Consider the second invariant
immediately after the loop on lines
1
–
4
.
If
\hat{s}\in W(\hat{p},\hat{q})
can be derived by (
base
),
then it is put on the
worklist
by that loop.
All other inferences have a premise
s^{\prime}\in W(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

for which
w(p^{\prime})
is undefined, since
w(\cdot)
is nowhere defined.
So initially, the invariant holds.
Noting that once
w(\hat{p})
is set, it will never be changed,
the invariant can be invalidated in only two ways.

(1)

(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})\in\textit{worklist}
is the item taken from the
worklist

on line
6
.
In this case,
either the algorithm aborts early on line
9
,
or we reach line
10
,
which ensures
w(\hat{p})=\hat{s}
and
n(\hat{p})=\hat{q}
since
\hat{s}
determines
\hat{q}
,
or we reach line
11
,
which assigns
w(\hat{p})=\hat{s}
and
n(\hat{p})=\hat{q}
.
So the invariant is maintained.

(2)

There is a premise
s^{\prime}\in W(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})

and
w(p^{\prime})
is assigned
s^{\prime}
on line
11
;
in that case,
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
must be
the most recent item taken from the
worklist
on line
6
.
This can only happen if
\hat{s}\in W(\hat{p},\hat{q})

is derived by one of the rules
(
step
E
) or (
step
F
) in the last step.

If the (
step
E
) rule is used,
let us assume that
s^{\prime}=s_{1}
,
p^{\prime}=p_{1}
and
q^{\prime}=q_{1}

(the case that
s^{\prime}=s_{2}
,
p^{\prime}=p_{2}
and
q^{\prime}=q_{2}
is completely analogous).
So
s_{1}\in W(p_{1},q_{1})
holds.
If the other premise
s_{2}\in W(p_{2},q_{2})
does not satisfy
w(p_{2})=s_{2}
,
then the invariant remains true.
If both
w(p_{1})=s_{1}
and
w(p_{2})=s_{2}
,
then
n(p_{1})=q_{1}
and
n(p_{2})=q_{2}

follow (because
\mathcal{N}
is deterministic);
since
\hat{s}\in W(\hat{p},\hat{q})
is derivable by (
step
E
),
there must also be rules
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to\hat{p}\in\mathcal{E}

(where
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is one of
(p_{1},q_{1})
or
(p_{2},q_{2})
)
and
q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to\hat{q}\in\mathcal{N}
.
Consequently,
(\hat{p}=p_{r},\hat{q}=q_{r},\hat{s}=s_{1}\circ s_{2})
will be added to the
worklist
on line
15
.

If the (
step
F
) is used,

\hat{q}=q^{\prime}
holds and
there must be a step
\hat{p}\to p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
;
hence
(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})
will be put on the
worklist
on line
17
.
∎

Lemma
\thethm
.

Whenever line
5
is reached in Listing
13
, we have

(1)

If
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}
or
s=w^{\prime}(p,q)
is defined,
then
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
or
s=\infty
and
|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1
.

(2)

Assume that
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
can be inferred using
an inference rule from Figure
12

with premises
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
.
Then
(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})\in\textit{worklist}
,

w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\hat{s}
,
w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\infty
,
or there is a premise
P_{i}=s^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
such that
w^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is
not equal to
s^{\prime}
or
\infty
.

Proof.

Consider the first invariant.
First note that
w^{\prime}(p,q)
is only updated on lines
10
and
12
.
In this case,
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}
was true
at the beginning of the loop,
so
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
or
s=\infty
and
|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1
.
This justifies setting
w^{\prime}(p,q)=s
on line
12
.
On line
10
, we additionally have
t\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
;
we conclude that
|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1

(justifying
w^{\prime}(p,q)=\infty
)
because either
s=\infty
, or
s\neq t
and
s,t\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
.

Hence we may focus on the items put on the
worklist
.
On line
4
, since
w(q)\in W(q,n(q))
and
p\uparrow q
,

w(q)\in W^{\prime}(p,n(q))
follows.
On line
18
, we have
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to p_{r}\in\mathcal{E}

and
q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q_{r}\in\mathcal{E}
.
Moreover, we have either
s_{1}^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p_{1},q_{1})
or
s_{1}^{\prime}=\infty
and
|W^{\prime}(p_{1},q_{1})|>1
; and
either
s_{2}^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})
or
s_{2}^{\prime}=\infty
and
|W^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})|>1
.
If neither
s_{1}^{\prime}=\infty
nor
s_{2}^{\prime}=\infty
,
then we have
s_{1}^{\prime}\circ s_{2}^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
by (
step
E
).
Otherwise, since we can derive
t_{1}\circ t_{2}\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
by (
step
E
)
for any
t_{1}\in W^{\prime}(p_{1},q_{1})
and
t_{2}\in W^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})
,

|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1
follows, and
s_{1}^{\prime}\circ s_{2}^{\prime}=\infty
.
So the invariant holds.
Finally, on line
20
, we have
p\to p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
,
and
either
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
or
s=\infty
and
|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1
.
In the former case,
s\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q)
by (
step
F
),
while in the latter case,
t\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q)
for any
t\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
;
either way, the invariant holds.

Next we consider the second invariant.
Immediately after the loop on lines
3
–
4
,
if
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
follows by (
base
′
) then

(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})
will be on the
worklist
.
For all other inferences of some
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
,
there is a premise
s^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
such that
w^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is undefined,
because initially,
w^{\prime}
is nowhere defined.
The invariant for
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
may be invalidated in three ways.

(1)

(\hat{p},\hat{q},\hat{s})
is
the item taken from the
worklist
on line
6
.
In this case, lines
7
–
12
ensure
that
w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\hat{s}
or
w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\infty

at the next loop iteration.

(2)

w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\hat{s}
holds and the value of
w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
is updated;
this may only happen on line
10
,
and the invariant still holds with
w^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})=\infty
.

(3)

There is a premise
s^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
,
and
w^{\prime}(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is set to
s^{\prime}
or
\infty
on line
10
or
12
.
This means that the item taken from the
worklist
on line
6

satisfies
p=p^{\prime}
,
q=q^{\prime}
, and
s=s^{\prime}
or
s=\infty
.
Note that
s=w^{\prime}(p,q)
holds at line
13
.

If (
step
{}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
) is used to infer
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
,
let us assume that
s^{\prime}=s_{1}
,
p^{\prime}=p_{1}
and
q^{\prime}=q_{1}
;
(the case that
s^{\prime}=s_{2}
,
p^{\prime}=p_{2}
and
q^{\prime}=q_{2}
is analogous).
If
w^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})
is not equal to
s_{2}
nor
\infty
,
then the invariant is maintained.
Note that we have
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to\hat{p}\in\mathcal{E}
and

q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to\hat{q}\in\mathcal{N}
,
with
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in\{(p_{1},q_{1}),(p_{2},q_{2})\}
.
Hence line
18
is reached with
p_{r}=\hat{p}
,
q_{r}=\hat{q}
.
At that point,
s_{1}=w^{\prime}(p_{1},q_{1})
,
s_{2}=w^{\prime}(p_{2},q_{2})
.
So either
s_{1}\circ s_{2}=\hat{s}
, or
s_{1}\circ s_{2}=\infty

so putting the item
(p_{r},q_{r},s_{1}\circ s_{2})
on the
worklist

restores the invariant.

If (
step
{}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
) is used to infer
\hat{s}\in W^{\prime}(\hat{p},\hat{q})
,
then
q^{\prime}=\hat{q}
and there must be a rule
p^{\prime}\to\hat{p}\in\mathcal{F}
.
Hence line
20
will put
(\hat{p},\hat{q},s)
with

s=\hat{s}
or
s=\infty
on the
worklist
,
restoring the invariant.
∎

Theorem
\thethm
.

The procedure in Figures
11
and
13
is correct
and takes
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3}\log\|\mathcal{R}\|)
time.

Proof.

Consider the check of the first
UNR
-condition (Listing
11
).
Note that by the first invariant of Lemma
5.3
,
the check on line
8
succeeds only
if the first
UNR
-condition is violated,
since at that point
s\in W(p,q)
,
t\in W(p,n(q))
and
s\neq t
,
so
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}p\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 29.28482pt\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-29.28482pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 24.8822pt\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-24.8822pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.7156pt\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.7156pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
and

s
and
t
are
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms.
When the main loop exits, the
worklist
is empty,
making the case that
(p,q,s)\in\textit{worklist}

in the second invariant impossible.
Therefore,
we can show by induction on the derivation
that for any derivation of
s\in W(p,q)

by the inference rules in Figure
10
,

w(p)=s
holds,
using the second invariant.
Consequently, the resulting partial function
w

witnesses the fact that the first
UNR
-condition holds.
Therefore, the check of the first
UNR
-condition is correct.

Now look at the check of the second
UNR
-condition (Listing
13
).
Using the first invariant of Lemma
5.3
,
we see that the check on line
13

succeeds only if the second
UNR
-condition is violated,
since at that point, either
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
and
s\neq w(p)
,
or
|W^{\prime}(p,q)|>1
,
ensuring that
W^{\prime}(p,q)
contains an element distinct from
w(p)
.
On the other hand,
if we reach line
21
,
the
worklist
is empty,
and by induction on the derivation we can show that
for all derivations of
s\in W^{\prime}(p,q)
using the rules in Figure
12
,
either
w^{\prime}(p,q)=s
or
w^{\prime}(p,q)=\infty
,
using the second invariant.
Furthermore, the check on line
13
has failed
for all defined values of
w^{\prime}(p,q)
,
which means that whenever both
w^{\prime}(p,q)
and
w(p)
are defined,
then they are equal; in particular,
w^{\prime}(p,q)\neq\infty
.
Therefore, in (
4
), if
w(p)
is defined,
we must have
t=w^{\prime}(p,q)=w(p)=s
,
and the second
UNR
-condition follows, establishing
UNR
by Lemma
5.1
.

Next we establish the complexity bound. Let
n=\|\mathcal{R}\|
.
We claim that the check of the first
UNR
-condition (Listing
11
)
takes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time.
First note that the precomputation (line
1
) can be performed cubic time.
Moreover, the bottom part of the main loop (lines
11
–
17
)
is executed at most
\mathcal{O}(n)
times, once for each possible value of
p
.
So even without indexing the rules of
\mathcal{E}
,
the bottom part takes at most
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}\log n)
time,
where the
\log n
factor stems from the query of
\mathcal{N}
and
the maintenance of maximal sharing when constructing
s_{1}\circ s_{2}
.
Furthermore, only
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
items are ever added to the
worklist
,
so the top part of the loop (lines
5
–
10
) also takes

\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time. Overall, the check of the first
UNR
-condition is
dominated by the cubic time precomputation.

For the complexity second
UNR
-condition (Listing
13
),
note that computation of
\uparrow
takes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time.
We focus on the main loop (lines
4
–
20
).
Because
w^{\prime}(p,q)
is updated at most twice for each combination
(p,q)
,
lines
13
–
20
are executed at most
\mathcal{O}(n)
times for
each possible value of
p
, for a total of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
times.
By indexing the rules of
\mathcal{E}
we
can perform the enumeration on line
16
in a total
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time,
accounting for
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
selected rules of
\mathcal{E}

(each of which is used for at most two values of
p
),
and
\mathcal{O}(n)
possible values for
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
,
depending on whether
(p,q)=(p_{1},q_{1})
or
(p,q)=(p_{2},q_{2})
.
By the same analysis, lines
17
–
18
are also executed
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time in total,
for a total runtime of
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\log n)

(as for the first check, the
\log n
factor
stems from the query of
\mathcal{N}

and the maximal sharing of
s_{1}\circ s_{2}
).
Lines
19
–
20
are also executed
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
times.
Overall at most
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
items are added to
worklist
,
so lines
6
–
14
,
which are executed once per
worklist
item,
take
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time.
In summary, the complexity is
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}\log n)
as claimed.
∎

6.
Deciding
NFP

In this section we show how to decide

NFP
for a finite ground TRS
\mathcal{R}
in
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
time.
As preprocessing, we curry the TRS to bound its arity
(Section
3.1
),
compute the automaton
\mathcal{N}
that accepts the
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal forms
(Section
3.3
),
the congruence closure
\mathcal{C}
for efficient convertibility checking
(Section
3.4
),
and the rewrite closure
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
that
allows testing reachability (Section
3.5
).

Remark
\thethm
.

The decision procedure for
NFP
is an extension of that for
UNC
,
so reading Section
4
first is recommended.

6.1.
Conditions for
NFP

For the analysis in this subsection,
we adapt the concept of top-stabilizable sides,
which was introduced for analyzing confluence of ground TRSs
[
3
,
6
,
9
]
.

Definition
\thethm
.

A left-hand side
\ell
of the transitions of
\mathcal{C}

is
top-stabilizable
if there is an
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal form
s
with

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}\ell
.

Remark
\thethm
.

A term
s
is top-stable (or root-stable)
if no reduction starting at
s
has a root step.
In the particular case of
\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
,
all rules have a side in
\Sigma^{[]}
.
So
s
is top-stable if there is no reduction from
s
to an element of
\Sigma^{[]}
.
By Lemma
3.5
,
any reduction
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{E}^{\pm}}^{*}}q\in\Sigma^{[]}

factors as
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}}^{*}}q
,
so
s
is top-stable if none of its
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal forms is in
\Sigma^{[]}
.
A term is top-stabilizable if it is convertible to a top-stable term.
In our setting,
convertibility is treated by the congruence closure
\mathcal{C}
,
which works over a different signature than the rewrite closure.
The connection is made by Lemma
3.4
,
which explains why the operation
(\cdot)_{\mathcal{R}}

features in Definition
6.1
.

Assume that
NFP
holds for
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
.
In particular,
UNC
holds, and by the reasoning from Section
4
,
there is a partial function
w(\cdot)

mapping
q\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}

to the unique
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form that reaches
q
by
\mathcal{C}
steps,
if such a normal form exists.
Because
NFP
holds,

s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
with
s,t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})
and

t
in
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form implies
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
.
The former is equivalent to
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}u\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
.
Let us assume that
u=q\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
.
This implies
t=w(q)
,
so by Lemma
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
we obtain
a term
s^{\prime}
with

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t=w(q)\qquad\text{and}\qquad s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 27.88205pt\hskip-27.88205pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-27.88205pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 23.56274pt\hskip-23.56274pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-23.56274pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 22.39615pt\hskip-22.39615pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}}^{*}$}\hskip-22.39615pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t

Assume that
s^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal form of
s
.
Note that if
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
(i.e.,
t
is not a constant),
then there are states
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}

such that
t_{1}=w(q_{1})
,
t_{2}=w(q_{2})
and

t\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
.
Further note that if
s^{\prime}=s^{\prime}_{1}\circ s^{\prime}_{2}
(i.e.,
s^{\prime}
is not a constant),
then we have states
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}
with

(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
,
which means that
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
is top-stabilizable.
Conversely,
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}
with
a top-stabilizable side
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
implies the existence of such
a term
s
with non-constant
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal form
s^{\prime}

(
s^{\prime}
exists by definition and we can let
s=s^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}
).
If
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma
then
s=s^{\prime}=t
.
We consider four remaining cases with
s^{\prime}\notin\Sigma
,
based on whether or not
s^{\prime}
and
t
are constants.

(1)

s^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{[]}
and
t\in\Sigma
.
Then
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}\cdot\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}t

(using that
\mathcal{F}
is reflexive and transitive).

(2)

s^{\prime}=s^{\prime}_{1}\circ s^{\prime}_{2}
, and
t\in\Sigma
.
We have a contradiction to
t\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}^{-}}^{*}}s^{\prime}
.

(3)

s^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{[]}
, and
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
.
Then there must be a rule
p^{\prime}_{1}\circ p^{\prime}_{2}\to p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
such that

p^{\prime}_{1}\circ p^{\prime}_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}p^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}s^{\prime}
,

q_{1}=(p^{\prime}_{1})_{\mathcal{R}}
and
q_{2}=(p^{\prime}_{2})_{\mathcal{R}}
.
(The latter two conditions are necessary for

t_{1}\circ t_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}p^{\prime}_{1}\circ p^{\prime}_{2}
to hold,
cf. Lemma
3.4
)

(4)

s^{\prime}=s^{\prime}_{1}\circ s^{\prime}_{2}
and
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
.
Then
s^{\prime}_{1}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t_{1}
,

s^{\prime}_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t_{2}
imply
p_{1}=q_{1}
and
p_{2}=q_{2}

by Lemma
3.4
.

From these four cases we obtain the following necessary conditions,
using the fact that
w(q)=t
.

Definition
\thethm
.

The
NFP
-conditions are

(1)

if
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{[]}
and
t=w(q)\in\Sigma
are constants,
then
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}t
.

(2)

if
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}
, and

w(q)\in\Sigma
is a constant,
then
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
is not top-stabilizable.

(3)

if
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{[]}
is a constant,

w(q_{1})\circ w(q_{2})
is a
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form, and

q_{1}\circ q_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}
,
then there is a rule
p^{\prime}_{1}\circ p^{\prime}_{2}\to p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
with

p^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}^{-}}}s^{\prime}
and
q_{1}=(p^{\prime}_{1})_{\mathcal{R}}
and
q_{2}=(p^{\prime}_{2})_{\mathcal{R}}
.

(4)

if
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}

with top-stabilizable
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
,
and
w(q_{1})\circ w(q_{2})
is a
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
-normal form, then

p_{1}=q_{1}
and
p_{2}=q_{2}
.

Lemma
\thethm
.

The
NFP
-conditions are necessary and sufficient for
NFP
to hold,
provided that
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
is
UNC
.

Proof.

Necessity has already be established.
Assume that
\mathcal{R}^{\circ}
is
UNC
and satisfies the
NFP
-conditions.
Let
s\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t
with
s,t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\circ})
and
t
in normal form.
We have
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}u\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t
.
Let
s^{\prime}
be an
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal form of
s

(note that each application of an
\mathcal{E}
-rule decreases the size of the term,
while
\mathcal{F}
-rules do not change the size of terms,
so
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
is terminating).
We have
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xleftrightarrow[\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}]{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\flat}}^{*}}t
, and
consequently
(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}u\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}(t)_{\mathcal{R}}=t

by Lemma
3.4
.
In the remainder of the proof,
we show that
s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}t
.
This will establish
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t

by Lemma
3.5
in conjunction with Proposition
3.2
:

s=s{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}s^{\prime}{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}=t

We proceed by induction on
t
.
If
u\notin\Sigma^{[]}
,
then either
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=u=t
are constants,
or

(s)_{\mathcal{R}}=(s_{1})_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(s_{2})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}u_{1}\circ u_{2}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t_{1}\circ t_{2}=t

for some terms
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
and we conclude by the induction hypotheses for
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
So assume that
u=q\in\Sigma^{[]}
.
Then we have
(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t=w(q)
.
We consider three cases.

(1)

If
(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}
and
t
are constants, then so is
s^{\prime}
.
Because
s^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{E}
-normal-form,
we must have
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{[]}
.
By the first
NFP
-condition,

s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}^{*}}s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{F}}}{\cdot}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}^{-}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{-}}}t
,
which implies
s\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{R}^{\circ}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}^{*}}t

by Lemma
3.5
and Proposition
3.2
.

(2)

If
(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}
is not constant,
then
s^{\prime}=s_{1}^{\prime}\circ s_{2}^{\prime}
for some terms
s_{1}^{\prime}
,
s_{2}^{\prime}
,
and there are
p_{1},p_{2}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
with

(s_{1}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(s_{2}^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}p_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
.
This means that
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
is a top-stabilizable side,
so
t
cannot be a constant by the second
NFP
-condition.
Hence there are terms
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
with
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
,
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
such that

t_{1}\circ t_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
.
By the fourth
NFP
-condition, we have
p_{1}=q_{1}
and
p_{2}=q_{2}
,
and consequently,
(s^{\prime}_{i})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q_{i}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t_{i}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. We conclude by the induction hypothesis.

(3)

If
(s^{\prime})_{\mathcal{R}}
is a constant, but
t
is not,
then there are terms
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
with
t=t_{1}\circ t_{2}
,
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
such that

t_{1}\circ t_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}q_{1}\circ q_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
.
By the third
NFP
-condition, we obtain
p^{\prime}_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}
such that

s^{\prime}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}}}p^{\prime}_{1}\circ p^{\prime}_{2}
and

(p^{\prime}_{i})=q_{i}\mathchoice{\xleftarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\mathrel{\hskip 16.20837pt\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-16.20837pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 13.26398pt\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-13.26398pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hskip 12.09738pt\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\vphantom{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}$}\hskip-12.09738pt\hbox{$\vphantom{\leftarrow}{}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\vphantom{*}}$}{\leftarrow}}}t_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
and we conclude by the induction hypothesis.
∎

6.2.
Computing Top-Stabilizable Sides

First note that for rules
c\to[c]_{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{C}
with
c\in\Sigma
,

c
is never top-stabilizable, since
(s)_{\mathcal{R}}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}c
implies
s=c
,
and
c\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{E}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}}[c]
.
So any top-stabilizable side must have shape
p\circ q
.
In order to compute the top-stabilizable sides,
let us first consider
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
reducible terms
of shape
p\circ q
with
p,q\in\Sigma^{[]}
.
Because
\mathcal{F}
is reflexive and transitive,
this means that there are rules
p\to p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and
q\to q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}

such that
p^{\prime}\circ q^{\prime}
is a left-hand side of
\mathcal{E}
.
We can compute

\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}=\{p\circ q\mid p,q\in\text{$\Sigma^{[]}$ and $p\circ q$ is in $\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}$-normal form}\}

as the complement of the
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-reducible terms;
the latter can be computed by enumerating the
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)

left-hand sides
p\circ q
of
\mathcal{E}
,
and the
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2})
possible pairs
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
,
taking
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{3})
time in total.
The size of
\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}
is
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|^{2})
.

Let
\mathsf{TS}(p\circ q)
denote the fact that
p\circ q
is a top-stabilizable side.
For convenience, we extend the notion to the right-hand sides of
\mathcal{C}
:

\mathsf{TS}(q)
for
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}
with
\mathsf{TS}(p_{1}\circ p_{2})
.
In this case, we call
q
a top-stabilizable constant.
The top-stabilizable constants and sides can be found using an
incremental computation.
Every
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{F}
-normal form
p\circ q\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}

for which
(p)_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(q)_{\mathcal{R}}
is a left-hand side of
\mathcal{C}

induces a top-stabilizable side
(p)_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(q)_{\mathcal{R}}
.
If
p_{1}\circ p_{2}
is top-stabilizable and
p_{1}\circ p_{2}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow[\mathcal{C}]{}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}}}q
, then

q
is a top-stabilizable constant.
For any top-stabilizable constant
p
,
p\circ q
,
q\circ p
for
constant
q\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
are also top-stabilizable.
Consequently, we obtain the following inference rules,
where
i\in\{1,2\}
.

\mathsf{TS}((p_{1})_{\mathcal{R}}\circ(p_{2})_{\mathcal{R}})p_{1}\circ p_{2}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}\qquad\mathsf{TS}(q)\lx@proof@logical@and p_{1}\circ p_{2}\to q\in\mathcal{C}\mathsf{TS}(p_{1}\circ p_{2})\qquad\mathsf{TS}(q_{1}\circ q_{2})\mathsf{TS}(q_{i})

Restricting to left-hand sides of
\mathcal{C}
,
there are
\mathcal{O}(\|\mathcal{R}\|)

instances of (
nf
), (
ts
0
),
(
ts
1
) and (
ts
2
).
Using Horn inference, they allow computing the top-stabilizable sides
in
